Critical

Chromium extension uses AI‑related branding to redirect browser search

Microsoft Security Blog
2026-06-29 10 use cases 3 techniques 5 kill-chain phases detected

A malicious Chromium-based extension that spoofs the AI-powered answer engine Perplexity AI redirects browser search traffic using MV3 APIs and intermediary infrastructure. The post Chromium extension uses AI‑related branding to redirect browser search appeared first on Microsoft Security Blog .

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Chromium extension uses AI‑related branding to redirect browser search
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Malicious Perplexity-spoofing extension ID flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd written to disk · Suspicious browser extension installation
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Search-interception traffic to Perplexity typosquat domain perplexity-ai[.]online · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs
Search-interception traffic to Perplexity typosquat domain perplexity-ai[.]online AICommand & Control High
Hunts browser connections/DNS resolutions to the attacker-controlled search-interception infrastructure (perplexity-ai.online and related oda.digital / extension.tilda.ws) used by the fake 'Search for perplexity ai' Chromium extension. Because the extension routes every Omnibox query and keystroke-level suggestion through this domain before redirecting to a real engine, any browser-originated traffic to these hosts is high-fidelity evidence of an infected endpoint. Rationale: Anchored on the exact interception domain perplexity-ai.online (confirmed by both the MSFT blog and The Hacker News) plus the pre-extracted secondary infra oda.digital / extension.tilda.ws, scoped to browser parent processes — a typosquat domain reached by a browser is near-zero-FP. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/malicious-perplexity-chrome-extension.html • https://chromewebstore.google.com/detail/search-for-perplexity-ai/flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd • https://www.crx4chrome.com/extensions/flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1176 T1185
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Web.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • SOC/IR analyst detonating the extension sample in a managed sandbox host
  • Web proxy / secure-web-gateway pre-fetching or URL-categorizing the domain on a user's behalf
  • Threat-intel enrichment or DNS-sinkhole tooling resolving the IOC for blocking
  • A user who clicked the lookalike landing page once but did not install (single browse vs. repeated query routing)
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsMpEng.exe","MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm which device/user generated the traffic and whether the 'Search for perplexity ai' extension (ID flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd) is installed; check if default search provider was overridden to perplexity-ai.online. 2) Contain: force-remove the extension via browser policy/ExtensionInstallBlocklist, reset the default search engine, and block perplexity-ai.online / oda.digital / extension.tilda.ws at proxy and DNS. 3) Hunt: pivot on all other hosts resolving these domains and review browsing/search history routed through the domain for sensitive query exposure during the exposure window.
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where RemoteUrl has_any ("perplexity-ai.online","oda.digital","extension.tilda.ws")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe","vivaldi.exe","chrome_proxy.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, RemoteUrl, RemoteIP, RemotePort, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
DnsEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Name has_any ("perplexity-ai.online","oda.digital","extension.tilda.ws")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, ClientIP, Name, QueryType, ResultCode, ResultCodeName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
title: DNS Query to Perplexity Typosquat Search-Interception Domain
id: 5b3e9a42-7c1f-4d2a-9f6e-2a1c8d4b7e90
description: Detects DNS resolution of perplexity-ai.online and related infrastructure used by a malicious Chromium extension spoofing Perplexity AI to intercept browser search queries.
references:
  - https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/29/chromium-extension-uses-airelated-branding-redirect-browser-search/
  - https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/malicious-perplexity-chrome-extension.html
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/06/29
tags:
  - attack.t1071.001
  - attack.t1176
  - attack.command_and_control
logsource:
  category: dns
detection:
  selection:
    QueryName|contains:
      - perplexity-ai.online
      - oda.digital
      - extension.tilda.ws
  condition: selection
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 @DestinationHostname:(*perplexity-ai.online* OR *oda.digital* OR extension.tilda.ws)
#event_simpleName=DnsRequest DomainName=/(perplexity-ai\.online|oda\.digital|extension\.tilda\.ws)/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, DomainName, ContextImageFileName], function=count())
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Network_Resolution.DNS where (DNS.query="*perplexity-ai.online*" OR DNS.query="*oda.digital*" OR DNS.query="extension.tilda.ws") by DNS.src DNS.query DNS.dest | `drop_dm_object_name(DNS)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime from datamodel=Network_Resolution.DNS where (DNS.query="*perplexity-ai.online*" OR DNS.query="*oda.digital*" OR DNS.query="extension.tilda.ws") by DNS.src DNS.query DNS.dest | `drop_dm_object_name(DNS)` | convert ctime(firstTime) ctime(lastTime) | sort - lastTime
Malicious Perplexity-spoofing extension ID flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd written to disk AIInstallation High
Hunts for the unique Chrome Web Store extension ID flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd ('Search for perplexity ai') appearing in browser extension folder paths or written files on managed endpoints. The extension ID is a globally unique 32-char identifier, so any file-system artifact bearing it is a deterministic indicator that the taken-down malicious search hijacker was installed. Rationale: The 32-char extension ID flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd is a globally unique store identifier (confirmed via the Chrome Web Store and crx4chrome listings), so its presence in any extension folder path is a deterministic install indicator with effectively no collisions. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/06/malicious-perplexity-chrome-extension.html • https://chromewebstore.google.com/detail/search-for-perplexity-ai/flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd • https://www.crx4chrome.com/extensions/flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd/
ATT&CKT1176 T1185
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Endpoint backup software capturing the Chrome 'User Data' profile directory that still contains the extension folder
  • IR/forensic collection tooling copying browser profiles for offline analysis
  • AV/EDR scanning or quarantining the extension folder (file-access touches the path)
  • Sync of an already-infected profile to a new device via Chrome profile sync before takedown
Suppression filter
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified") and InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsMpEng.exe","MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the ActionType (FileCreated = fresh install vs FileDeleted = remediation) and identify the user profile and browser hosting the extension folder. 2) Contain: remove the extension, push it to the browser ExtensionInstallBlocklist policy, and reset the hijacked default search provider; block perplexity-ai.online at DNS/proxy. 3) Hunt: correlate with UC1 network telemetry to confirm queries were exfiltrated, and sweep the fleet for the same extension ID to find all installs.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FolderPath has "flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, FileOriginUrl, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFilePath has "flkebkiofojicogddingbdmcmkpbplcd"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, DvcAction, TargetFilePath, TargetFileName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
Network connections to article IPs / domains Command & Control High
Outbound traffic to attacker infrastructure named in the article.
ATT&CKT1071
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic Web Network_Resolution.DNS DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where RemoteIP in ("") or RemoteUrl has_any ("perplexity-ai.online", "oda.digital", "extension.tilda.ws")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, RemoteIP, RemotePort, RemoteUrl,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
let BadIPs     = dynamic([""]);
let BadDomains = dynamic(["perplexity-ai.online", "oda.digital", "extension.tilda.ws"]);
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where DstIpAddr in (BadIPs)
   or Url has_any (BadDomains)
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber, Url, NetworkProtocol
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Infostealer — non-browser process accessing browser cookie/login DBs Actions on Objectives High
Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies SQLite from Chrome/Edge/Firefox.
ATT&CKT1539 T1555.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where FolderPath has_any (@"\Google\Chrome\User Data\", @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\", @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\")
| where FileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FolderPath, FileName, ActionType

// ASIM ImFileEvent — covers MDE, Sysmon, AuditD, Defender for Cloud, etc.
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where TargetFilePath has_any (@"\Google\Chrome\User Data\",
                                  @"\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\",
                                  @"\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\")
| where TargetFileName in~ ("Login Data","Cookies","logins.json","cookies.sqlite")
| where ActingProcessName !in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName,
          TargetFilePath, TargetFileName, EventType

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime max(_time) as lastTime
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where (Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Google\Chrome\User Data\*\Cookies*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.process_name, Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.user
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc

``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] The Gentlemen ransomware deployment via deploy_gpo.ps1 / NETLOGON staging · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft)
The Gentlemen network sniffing via netsh trace capture redirected to ADMIN$ share AIReconnaissance High
Hunts The Gentlemen's reconnaissance technique of running netsh trace packet capture and writing the .etl output across the network to a remote host's ADMIN$ share. The redirect of a netsh trace start/stop session to \\<ip>\ADMIN$\ is highly specific to this actor's credential-harvesting-from-the-wire workflow. Rationale: Built from the article's exact commands 'netsh trace start capture=yes report=no filemode=circular overwrite=yes maxSize=4 > \\<target IP>\ADMIN$\{RANDOM}' and the matching 'netsh trace stop' redirect. netsh trace + ADMIN$ redirect is rare in benign admin use, making this high-fidelity. Cross-checked against: • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/i/unmasking-the-gentlemen-ransomware.html • https://www.huntress.com/blog/the-gentlemen-ransomware-defense-evasion-ttps
ATT&CKT1040 T1059.003
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Network engineer running netsh trace for legitimate troubleshooting and saving to a local file (no ADMIN$ redirect)
  • Endpoint support tooling that captures ETL traces locally
  • Microsoft support diagnostic scripts (TSS toolkit) invoking netsh trace
  • Vendor performance-monitoring agents starting circular ETW captures
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("\\TSS\\", "TSSv2") and AccountName endswith "admin")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm the redirect target — a \\<remote-ip>\ADMIN$ path means cross-host write, not local troubleshooting. Identify the executing account and whether it is an admin/service account that does not normally run netsh. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the source host, reset the credentials of the executing account, and pull the dropped .etl from the target ADMIN$ share for analysis (it may contain cleartext creds). 3) HUNT: Search for SharpADWS/NetScan/Advanced IP Scanner activity, the deploy_gpo.ps1 script, and connections to 81.177.215.15:9443 on the same hosts.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "netsh" and ProcessCommandLine has "trace"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("capture=yes", @"\ADMIN$\")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFolderPath
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where not(SubjectUserName endswith "$")
| where CommandLine has "netsh" and CommandLine has "trace"
| where CommandLine has_any ("capture=yes", "\\ADMIN$\\")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

The Gentlemen Go backdoor C2 beacon to 81.177.215.15 on TCP/9443 AICommand & Control Medium
Alerts on any host establishing an outbound connection to the confirmed Gentlemen Go-backdoor C2 IP 81.177.215.15 on port 9443, the Yamux-multiplexed persistent channel used for remote command execution and SOCKS proxying. Single confirmed C2 indicator deployed a day before ransomware. Rationale: Uses the article's explicit C2 endpoint 81.177.215.15:9443 for the Yamux-based Go backdoor. A hardcoded malicious IP+port is a high-confidence, near-zero-FP network IOC. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/05/28/the-gentlemen-ransomware-dissecting-a-self-propagating-go-encryptor/ • https://s2w.inc/en/resource/detail/1053
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1571 T1090
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researchers/sandbox detonating the sample from corporate ranges
  • Threat-intel scanning or Shodan-style internet measurement hitting the IP
  • Reused VPS IP later assigned to a benign service (validate against current TI reputation)
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Identify the InitiatingProcess making the connection — a Go binary in an unusual path (Temp/Public/AppData) deployed ~1 day prior is the backdoor. Capture its SHA256 against the article's hash list. 2) CONTAIN: Network-isolate the host, block 81.177.215.15 at the perimeter, and kill/quarantine the implant binary. 3) HUNT: Look for WMI query 'SELECT UUID FROM Win32_ComputerSystemProduct', cmd.exe /c children of the implant (operator command 'c'), SOCKS proxy listeners, and expect ransomware staging within ~24h.

The Gentlemen BYOVD vulnerable driver drop/load (EDR-killer drivers) AIInstallation High
Hunts for the specific vulnerable kernel drivers The Gentlemen abuse in their BYOVD/GentleKiller workflow to terminate security products. Matches on the named .sys files being written or loaded, e.g. biontdrv.sys, wamsdk.sys, ProcessMonitorDriver.sys. Rationale: Driver names taken verbatim from the article's vulnerable-driver table (Safetica, WatchDog, Paragon biontdrv, inpoutx64, etc.). ESET's GentleKiller research corroborates these as the actor's BYOVD arsenal. Medium FP because inpoutx64.sys/biontdrv.sys also appear in legitimate software. Cross-checked against: • https://www.welivesecurity.com/en/eset-research/killing-me-gently-inside-gentlemens-edr-killer-framework/ • https://www.bleepingcomputer.com/news/security/gentlemen-ransomware-uses-multiple-edr-killers-to-disable-defenses/
ATT&CKT1562.001 T1068
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • inpoutx64.sys loaded by legitimate RGB-lighting/overclocking utilities
  • biontdrv.sys present from a genuine Paragon Partition Manager install
  • gamedriverx64.sys from a legitimately installed game with anti-cheat
  • Security product (Safetica) itself loading ProcessMonitorDriver.sys in an authorized deployment
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFolderPath !has @"\Program Files\" and InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("msiexec.exe","setup.exe","paragon.exe")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Check the signing status and the parent process that wrote/loaded the driver — a driver dropped to a non-standard path by a non-installer process is malicious; a vendor MSI installer is likely benign. Confirm whether the matching legitimate product is actually installed. 2) CONTAIN: Isolate the host, stop the driver service, and quarantine the .sys + the loading process; verify EDR/AV agents are still running. 3) HUNT: Correlate with Set-MpPreference tampering, OpenArk64/PowerRun.exe execution, and the 81.177.215.15 C2 on the same host to confirm Gentlemen activity.

The Gentlemen impair defenses via Set-MpPreference exclusions and AV-removal tooling AIInstallation High
Detects The Gentlemen's defense-evasion step that disables Defender real-time/CFA protection, adds whole-drive exclusions via Set-MpPreference/Add-MpPreference, and runs known AV-removal utilities (kavrmvr.exe, OpenArk64, PowerRun.exe, Allpatch2.exe). Pairs the Go-implant PowerShell cmdlets with named tampering binaries. Rationale: Combines the article's verbatim Go-implant cmdlets (Set-MpPreference -DisableRealtimeMonitoring, -EnableControlledFolderAccess Disabled, Add-MpPreference -ExclusionProcess/-ExclusionPath 'C:\') with named tools kavrmvr.exe (Kaspersky uninstaller) and OpenArk64; PowerRun.exe/Allpatch2.exe added from corroborating Trend Micro reporting on the same actor. Cross-checked against: • https://www.trendmicro.com/en_us/research/25/i/unmasking-the-gentlemen-ransomware.html • https://www.huntress.com/blog/the-gentlemen-ransomware-defense-evasion-ttps
ATT&CKT1562.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT admin legitimately adding a Defender exclusion for a line-of-business app (single ExclusionPath, not C:\)
  • Sanctioned migration off Kaspersky running kavrmvr.exe with change-ticket approval
  • Performance team disabling Real-Time Monitoring temporarily during a benchmark
  • Software packaging/SCCM scripts setting exclusions for an installer
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has "ExclusionPath" and ProcessCommandLine !has @"'C:\'" and ProcessCommandLine !has "DisableRealtimeMonitoring" and InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("ccmexec.exe","tiworker.exe"))
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Inspect the exclusion scope — a whole-drive 'C:\' exclusion or DisableRealtimeMonitoring chained with ExclusionProcess is malicious; a narrow app path may be benign. Identify the parent process (Go implant / cmd.exe /c). 2) CONTAIN: Re-enable Defender real-time protection and CFA, remove rogue exclusions, isolate the host, and reset the executing account. 3) HUNT: Pivot to BYOVD driver loads, registry Defender-policy keys, deploy_gpo.ps1, and the 81.177.215.15 C2 to scope the intrusion.

| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

The Gentlemen Windows Defender disabled via Policy registry keys AIInstallation High
Detects The Gentlemen disabling Defender by writing the policy registry values DisableAntiSpyware / DisableBehaviorMonitoring / DisableOnAccessProtection / DisableScanOnRealtimeEnable = 1 under the Windows Defender policy hive (via .reg import). Distinct registry-telemetry pivot from the PowerShell-based tampering. Rationale: Registry keys and values are taken verbatim from the article's exported .reg block under HKLM\SOFTWARE\Policies\Microsoft\Windows Defender. Value-name + data=1 under the policy hive is high-fidelity tampering. Falcon left empty: these policy keys are not reliably captured by the AsepValueUpdate (autostart) event the FDR exposes. Cross-checked against: • https://www.huntress.com/blog/the-gentlemen-ransomware-defense-evasion-ttps • https://s2w.inc/en/resource/detail/1053
ATT&CKT1562.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Registry
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Group Policy legitimately disabling Defender on hosts managed by a third-party AV (DisableAntiSpyware pushed by GPO)
  • Gold-image build process turning off Defender before installing the enterprise AV
  • Admin troubleshooting an AV conflict by temporarily setting DisableBehaviorMonitoring
  • MDM/SCCM baseline applying a Defender configuration
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("gpscript.exe","svchost.exe","ccmexec.exe","WmiPrvSE.exe")
Response runbook
1) TRIAGE: Confirm whether a sanctioned GPO/MDM pushed the value (InitiatingProcess = svchost/gpscript) versus an interactive process like reg.exe/regedit/cmd.exe run by a user. Check if a different enterprise AV is the intended owner. 2) CONTAIN: Revert the registry values, force a gpupdate, isolate the host if the writer was interactive/non-admin. 3) HUNT: Correlate same-host BYOVD driver loads, Set-MpPreference tampering, and C2 to 81.177.215.15:9443; expect ransomware staging shortly after.
DeviceRegistryEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("RegistryValueSet","RegistryKeyCreated")
| where RegistryKey has @"\Policies\Microsoft\Windows Defender"
| where RegistryValueName in~ ("DisableAntiSpyware","DisableBehaviorMonitoring","DisableOnAccessProtection","DisableScanOnRealtimeEnable")
| where RegistryValueData == "1"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, RegistryKey, RegistryValueName, RegistryValueData, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc

| where RegistryValueData in ("1","0x1","DWORD (0x00000001)")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, RegistryKey, RegistryValue, RegistryValueData, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine, ActorUsername
| order by TimeGenerated desc

The Gentlemen ransomware deployment via deploy_gpo.ps1 / NETLOGON staging AIActions on Objectives High
Detects The Gentlemen's domain-wide lateral movement: execution of the custom deploy_gpo.ps1 GPO-deployment script and staging of the ransomware executable on the NETLOGON share for simultaneous distribution to domain hosts. Rationale: Uses the article's named lateral-movement script deploy_gpo.ps1 and the NETLOGON-share distribution method. The deploy_gpo.ps1 filename is unique to this actor; NETLOGON-staged .exe execution corroborated by SOC Prime and Trend Micro GPO-abuse reporting. Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment

  • Software-distribution tooling that copies installers into SYSVOL/NETLOGON

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["3B46A729DB7AE6AF8B19711C9452194D", "02944C8A5535CDB5B2CBB893DB2D5ACF", "10CA9A4040001560D053B7E7885C1B95", "3C471EBC947CDF32240A90FFADF49B13", "4BE8BB62F0EBBCF4CE52C35AB6F794F5", "53C616677BC7E2A0A03127F19166D007", "5C3B9821FC82A9028CB63B9671950919", "5F0B2C6D9F442754258BF4DD841C8341", "608FAF58353B65C45EF9833358AC3787", "6AE7C9A7EA0B8C40A64225734F6BD01D", "846DC77C1246DB20D976346E0E359502", "ADAC9984B3CC43D66A0D33079BBEC299", "AE0E536766788478263BF448A9381641", "B3E418D30312C1B2C58A791286868F42", "C2764744DCB4B0E1DB79CA1E8BF65368", "D12A5B36DD00586CC374A1CAE43EFED4", "D2F72897E8986303D5567EB2384932B8", "DE1522F9219497632F30F8A6E72F26B6", "FDAE2BEB813778B4540A997706862096", "B9986A0F1F1F1A798DC3F0C59A80A1A3", "554E699C96B332468F1AE69C1AE81EF9", "5761BD63DA03686FC480245DA7BD1E9F", "B6B51508AD6F462C45FE102C85D246C8", "8F0577D28C4FF5F71B149F444BFABA8E", "525EF6014F0EF20E44FE47C1D9980B69", "407B6A136BBAA7172EB44EF9D08BB58A", "9321A61A25C7961D9F36852ECAA86F55", "73F0A8C3EA794A04E80C32038249F044", "EEF8A950952696B018AA9C6DA2F5D7AD", "EDB1C480295250DD1A38F3AA1357DEAE", "5537C708EDB9A2C21F88E34E8A0F1744"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Inside the inbox: Why cybercriminals want to break into your email account

ESET WeLiveSecurity
2026-06-29 4 use cases 3 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

Your inbox is an identity system all of its own: whoever owns it may own a lot more

ATT&CKT1190 T1566 T1566.001
Click any ATT&CK pill below to open it on the Matrix

Likely
Phase 7
Actions on Objectives
—
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha)
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
Email attachment opened from external sender Delivery High
Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.

title: FakeCaptcha / ClickFix - Clipboard-Injected PowerShell from Explorer
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d06
status: experimental
description: |
    "FakeCaptcha" / "ClickFix" social-engineering: malicious site asks
    user to press Win+R and paste a payload. The result is
    powershell/mshta launched directly from explorer.exe with iex /
    base64 / downloadstring in the command line.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/001/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1059.001
    - attack.t1204.002
    - attack.execution

    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_parent:
        ParentImage|endswith:
            - '\explorer.exe'
            - '\RuntimeBroker.exe'
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
    selection_cmd:
        CommandLine|contains:
            - 'iex'
            - 'invoke-expression'
            - 'frombase64'
            - 'downloadstring'
            - 'hxxp'
            - 'curl '
            - 'wget '

    - IT-driven scripted automation that admins paste-launch

        OR Processes.process="*FromBase64*" OR Processes.process="*DownloadString*"
        OR Processes.process="*hxxp*" OR Processes.process="*curl*" OR Processes.process="*wget*")

CISA KEV: CVE-2026-48558 — SimpleHelp Authentication Bypass Vulnerability

CISA KEV
2026-06-29 1 use case 0 techniques 2 kill-chain phases detected

SimpleHelp contains an authentication bypass vulnerability in the OIDC authentication flow. When OIDC authentication is configured, identity tokens submitted during login are accepted without verifying their cryptographic signature. In a vulnerable configuration, a remote, unauthenticated attacker can submit a forged token containing arbitrary identity claims to obtain a fully authenticated technician session. In some configurations, this may also allow bypass of multi-factor authentication. Vendor: SimpleHelp , Product: SimpleHelp. Federal patch due: 2026-07-02.

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access

    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'

            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'

            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'

            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef") or SHA1 in~ ("636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef") or MD5 in~ ("636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef") or SHA1 in~ ("636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef") or MD5 in~ ("636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["636f4a971653ce3f5272fee99dc85c0bd5f923ef"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\*\Login Data*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")
      AND NOT Filesystem.process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe","brave.exe","opera.exe")

| extend AppName = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].displayName),
         AppId   = tostring(parse_json(tostring(TargetResources))[0].id),
         User    = tostring(parse_json(tostring(InitiatedBy)).user.userPrincipalName)
| project TimeGenerated, OperationName, User, AppName, AppId, Result
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad") or SHA1 in~ ("1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad") or MD5 in~ ("1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad") or SHA1 in~ ("1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad") or MD5 in~ ("1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["1c7d3f9cd384854bf72c127d230bdb32299475ad"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.
ATT&CKT1195.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes DeviceProcessEvents
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","cmd.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","mshta.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe","wmic.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine
// Replace `"setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe"` with the vendor binaries observed in the
// article (matching shape with the Defender query).
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where EventID == 4688
| where Account !endswith "$"
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("setup.exe","installer.exe","update.exe")
| where NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\rundll32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\regsvr32.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wmic.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\bitsadmin.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children Exploitation Medium
Supply-chain trojan signal: legitimate signed binary spawning script interpreters or exotic LOLBins.

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["221c45a790dec2a296af57969e1165a16f8f49733aeab64c0bbd768d9943badf", "4a8860240e4231c3a74c81949be655a28e096a7d72f38fbe84e5b37636b98417", "ae70dd4f6bc0d1c8c2848e4e6b51934626c4818dcb5af99d080ddbd7dc337185", "2553649f2322049666871cea80a5d0d6adc700ca", "d6f3f62fd3b9f5432f5782b62d8cfd5247d5ee71", "07d889e2dadce6f3910dcbc253317d28ca61c766"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

FortiBleed password-spray success burst against internet-facing Fortinet/edge auth AIExploitation Medium
Hunts a single source IP producing a high volume of failed authentications against a Fortinet appliance (SSL-VPN / admin login) followed by a successful login in the same window — the exact 'successful logins shortly after large-volume password failure events' pattern Unit 42 flags for the FortiBleed credential-spraying campaign. Rationale: Implements Unit 42's explicit FortiBleed guidance — audit remote-access logs for successful logins shortly after large-volume failure events — scoped to Fortinet auth via the CIM app values / DeviceVendor='Fortinet'. Threshold + same-window success join is what separates spray-then-compromise from routine VPN typos. Cross-checked with SOCRadar/SecurityWeek FortiBleed reporting and Arctic Wolf's CVE-2024-55591 analysis. Cross-checked against: • https://socradar.io/blog/what-is-fortibleed/ • https://www.securityweek.com/fortibleed-86000-fortinet-device-credentials-compromised/ • https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/cve-2024-55591/
ATT&CKT1110.003 T1110.004 T1133
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate remote worker repeatedly fat-fingering an SSL-VPN password then succeeding
  • Misconfigured VPN client or saved-credential loop retrying stale creds before a successful re-auth
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanner (Qualys/Tenable) credential checks against the appliance
  • Shared NAT egress IP where many staff log in (inflated failure+success counts from one SourceIP)
  • Password-rotation day causing widespread first-attempt failures followed by success
Suppression filter
| where SourceIP !in ("<populate watchlist: known scanner + corp NAT egress IPs via a Sentinel watchlist join, not inline>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the CompromisedUser + SourceIP; geolocate SourceIP and check whether the success used MFA and from a known ASN/country. Inspect the failed-user set — many distinct users from one IP = spray, not a typo. 2) Contain: if SourceIP is foreign/unrecognized, force-disable the compromised admin/VPN account, kill active SSL-VPN tunnels, block the SourceIP at the edge, and rotate the credential. 3) Hunt: from that SourceIP/account pivot to post-auth config changes (new admin accounts, sslvpn user groups), jsconsole admin logins, and any config download — feed into the FortiBleed jsconsole and rogue-admin UCs.
let lookback = 7d;
let window = 1h;
let Failed = CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Activity has "login" and (EventOutcome in~ ("failure","failed") or Message has_any ("login failed","Login failed","authentication failed","ssl-login-fail"))
| summarize Failures = count(), TargetUsers = dcount(DestinationHostName) by SourceIP, bin(TimeGenerated, window);
let Success = CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Activity has "login" and (EventOutcome in~ ("success","succeeded") or Message has_any ("login successful","SSL tunnel established"))
| summarize Successes = count(), CompromisedUser = make_set(DestinationHostName, 20) by SourceIP, bin(TimeGenerated, window);
Failed
| where Failures >= 30   // 30 = empirical spray burst floor per source per hour
| join kind=inner Success on SourceIP, TimeGenerated
| project TimeGenerated, SourceIP, Failures, TargetUsers, Successes, CompromisedUser
| order by Failures desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`
| stats sum(eval(if(action="failure",count,0))) as failures, sum(eval(if(action="success",count,0))) as successes, dc(user) as targeted_users, values(eval(if(action="success",user,null()))) as compromised_user, values(dest) as dest by src, _time
| where failures >= 30 and successes >= 1
| sort - failures
| tstats summariesonly=false count from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.app IN ("fortios","fortigate","fortiproxy","ftnt-fortigate","sslvpn-plain","forticlient_ems") by Authentication.src, Authentication.dest, Authentication.user, Authentication.action, _time span=1h
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`

| where failures >= 30 and successes >= 1
| sort - failures
FortiGate jsconsole / loopback admin login (CVE-2024-55591 auth-bypass exploitation) AIExploitation High
Hunts the high-fidelity exploitation signature of CVE-2024-55591: administrative logins attributed to the 'jsconsole' interface and/or successful super-admin logins sourced from the device loopback/local IP — the artifact the Node.js websocket auth-bypass leaves when an unauthenticated attacker forges super_admin access on FortiOS/FortiProxy. Rationale: Uses the two corroborated CVE-2024-55591 exploitation artifacts — `ui=jsconsole` in the admin-login record (matched in CommonSecurityLog Message) and super-admin logins from the device loopback/local IP (CIM Authentication.src=127.0.0.1) — reported by Arctic Wolf, watchTowr ('I Am The Super_Admin Now') and Trustwave. Both are near-zero in benign FortiGate operation. Cross-checked against: • https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/cve-2024-55591/ • https://labs.watchtowr.com/get-fortirekt-i-am-the-super_admin-now-fortios-authentication-bypass-cve-2024-55591/ • https://trustwave.com/en-us/resources/blogs/spiderlabs-blog/cve-2024-55591-fortinet-fortios-fortiproxy-zero-day
ATT&CKT1190 T1078.001 T1556
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Admin legitimately running CLI commands through the FortiGate GUI 'CLI console' widget (also tagged jsconsole) — distinguish by source being a real admin IP, not loopback
  • Automation/orchestration tooling (Ansible/FortiManager) driving CLI via the local console
  • Internal penetration test replicating CVE-2024-55591
  • Health-check scripts hitting the management interface from a jump host
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm whether the jsconsole/loopback login corresponds to a real admin session — legitimate jsconsole use originates from an authenticated GUI session with a genuine admin SourceIP; exploitation shows loopback/local src and no preceding interactive auth. Check FortiOS version against the CVE-2024-55591 fixed builds. 2) Contain: isolate the device's management interface from the internet, disable/rotate all admin accounts, and capture the running + startup config for IR. 3) Hunt: enumerate admin accounts and sslvpn user groups created/modified after the first jsconsole login, look for `--userfrom`-style audit-record tampering, and any config download/exfil to chain with the rogue-admin and FortiBleed config-extraction UCs.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Message has "jsconsole"
| where Message has_any ("login","logged in","logged-in","admin")
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceProduct, DeviceExternalID, SourceIP, AdminUser = SourceUserName, DestinationHostName, Activity, EventOutcome, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc

FortiGate rogue super_admin / SSL-VPN account creation following CVE-2024-55591 exploit AIInstallation High
Hunts post-exploitation persistence for the FortiBleed/CVE-2024-55591 chain: creation of new FortiGate administrator accounts (often random usernames) or new SSL-VPN local users / user groups via config changes. This is how attackers convert a one-shot auth bypass into durable, high-privilege access for offline-cracked credential re-use. Rationale: Targets the documented CVE-2024-55591 post-exploit actions — new admin accounts with random names, new SSL-VPN local users and groups — via Fortinet config-change logs (cfgpath system.admin / user.group) and the CIM Change datamodel (action=created, object_category=user). Account creation on an edge appliance is rare and tightly change-controlled, so FP is low. Corroborated by Arctic Wolf and Cyble. Cross-checked against: • https://arcticwolf.com/resources/blog/cve-2024-55591/ • https://cyble.com/blog/cve-2024-55591-the-fortinet-flaw-putting-critical-systems-at-risk/
ATT&CKT1136 T1098 T1133
Data sourcesChange.All_Changes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Planned admin onboarding by the network team during a change window
  • FortiManager/automation pushing a config template that provisions admin or VPN users
  • Legitimate SSL-VPN user-group expansion during an access-request rollout
  • MSP/managed-SOC provisioning a monitoring admin account
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the ActorAdmin and SourceIP that created the new object; correlate against an authorized change ticket and verify the actor session was not a jsconsole/loopback exploit login. Flag random-looking usernames. 2) Contain: delete or disable the unauthorized admin/VPN account and group, terminate its sessions, and rotate credentials for every legitimate admin on the device. 3) Hunt: review whether the new account granted VPN access, established tunnels into internal networks, or downloaded the device config; pivot on its SourceIP across other edge devices to scope the FortiBleed campaign footprint.
CommonSecurityLog
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where DeviceVendor =~ "Fortinet"
| where Message has_any ("system.admin","user.local","user.group")
| where Message has_any ("super_admin","prof_admin","accprofile","sslvpn")
| where Message has_any ("Object attribute configured","Added","cfgattr","New","create")
| project TimeGenerated, DeviceProduct, SourceIP, ActorAdmin = SourceUserName, NewObject = DestinationHostName, Activity, Message
| order by TimeGenerated desc
