FortiBleed MSSQL login-failure burst then success (internet-exposed SQL spraying) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts the FortiBleed campaign's parallel targeting of internet-exposed MSSQL: a single client IP generating a burst of SQL Server login failures (error 18456) across one or many logins within a short window — the spray signature Unit 42 observed against MSSQL devices alongside Fortinet and Sophos. Rationale: Covers the article's explicit observation that the FortiBleed actors also targeted MSSQL devices. Pivots on SQL Server error 18456 (login failed) parsed for CLIENT IP and login name in Sentinel's Event table, with a Defender DeviceLogonEvents fallback for Windows-auth brute force against the SQL host and a CIM Authentication path for Splunk. Threshold-based, so framed as a burst detector. Cross-checked against: • https://socradar.io/blog/what-is-fortibleed/ • https://www.securityweek.com/fortibleed-86000-fortinet-device-credentials-compromised/
ATT&CKT1110.003 T1078
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Application/service account with a stale connection string hammering the DB after a password change
  • Linked-server or ETL job misconfigured with wrong credentials retrying in a tight loop
  • Monitoring agent (SCOM/SolarWinds) probing SQL with expired creds
  • Authenticated vulnerability scanner credential audit against the SQL instance
  • Developer load-test pointing at the wrong SQL host
Suppression filter
| where AccountName !in~ ("sqlmonitor","svc-etl","svc-backup")   // tune to your known SQL service accounts
Response runbook
1) Triage: resolve ClientIP — internal app server vs external/unknown. Inspect the targeted login set: many distinct logins from one IP = spray; a single service account = likely stale credential. Check for any 18454/successful login from the same ClientIP right after the burst. 2) Contain: if external, block the ClientIP at the firewall, confirm the SQL instance is not directly internet-exposed, and disable SQL-auth logins that were sprayed; enforce that MSSQL sits behind the VPN/jump box. 3) Hunt: if a success followed, review that login's subsequent activity (xp_cmdshell, new SQL logins, linked-server abuse) and pivot the ClientIP across Fortinet edge logs to tie it to the broader FortiBleed spraying infrastructure.
DeviceLogonEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ActionType == "LogonFailed"
| where LogonType in ("Network","Batch")
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| summarize Failures = count(), TargetedAccounts = dcount(AccountName), Accounts = make_set(AccountName, 30), FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp) by RemoteIP, DeviceName, bin(Timestamp, 30m)
| where Failures >= 30   // 30 failed network logons / 30 min from one source = spray
| order by Failures desc
let lookback = 7d;
let window = 30m;
Event
| where TimeGenerated > ago(lookback)
| where Source == "MSSQLSERVER"
| where EventID == 18456   // SQL Server: Login failed
| extend LoginUser = extract(@"Login failed for user '([^']+)'", 1, RenderedDescription)
| extend ClientIP = extract(@"\[CLIENT: ([0-9A-Fa-f\.:]+)\]", 1, RenderedDescription)
| where isnotempty(ClientIP)
| summarize Failures = count(), TargetedLogins = dcount(LoginUser), Logins = make_set(LoginUser, 30) by ClientIP, Computer, bin(TimeGenerated, window)
| where Failures >= 30
| order by Failures desc

| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url

| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-48755")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-48755")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

| order by Timestamp desc

          FileName, ActionType, InitiatingProcessFileName,

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb") or SHA1 in~ ("40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb") or MD5 in~ ("40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb") or SHA1 in~ ("40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb") or MD5 in~ ("40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["40dd4f151d52c06b178482aa2518abfb9df3e6fb"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access

    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'

        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'

        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high

| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName,
          CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.

    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/

date: 2026/05/02

    - attack.initial-access

        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'

            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'

        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'

Schneider FLM CVE-2024-2658: rogue openssl.cnf planted in hardcoded cygwin search path AIInstallation High
Hunts creation of the attacker-reconstructed hardcoded directory tree (C:\cygwin\home\nightly\LMADMI~1.4\...) and/or the rogue openssl.cnf that lmadmin.exe blindly loads. A clean Schneider Electric FLM install never creates this cygwin path, so any non-SYSTEM write to it is the CVE-2024-2658 primitive being staged. Rationale: Anchored on the article's verbatim hardcoded path C:\cygwin\home\nightly\LMADMI~1.4\...\openssl\openssl.cnf; the article states a clean FLM install never creates this folder, so a non-SYSTEM write here is a near-zero-FP CVE-2024-2658 stager. Cross-checked Flexera and CISA advisories confirming the C:\cygwin path and non-admin write primitive. Cross-checked against: • https://community.flexera.com/s/article/cve-2024-2658-flexnet-publisher-potential-local-privilege-escalation-issue • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/ics-advisories/icsa-25-037-02 • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2024-2658
ATT&CKT1574 T1574.007
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Administrator legitimately installs Cygwin under C:\cygwin for engineering tooling
  • Backup/restore software rehydrating an old FLM install tree
  • Schneider Electric installer or updater re-writing contrib\openssl content under a SYSTEM context
  • Security/vuln scanner deliberately planting a benign openssl.cnf as a CVE-2024-2658 test
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessAccountName in~ ("system","local service","network service") or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\Schneider Electric\" or InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("setup.exe","msiexec.exe"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the writing account (InitiatingProcessAccountName) is a low-privileged interactive user, not SYSTEM/installer; open the created openssl.cnf and inspect the [engine] section for a dynamic_path pointing at a writeable DLL. 2) Contain: delete the rogue C:\cygwin tree, tighten ACLs on C:\ root to deny Authenticated Users folder creation, and block restart of the lmadminSchneider service until patched. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same host/user for the referenced DLL (e.g. C:\Users\Public\*.dll), for lmadmin.exe image loads from user-writeable paths, and for any lmadmin.exe child processes indicating the payload already fired.
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")
| where FolderPath has @"cygwin\home\nightly" or (FileName =~ "openssl.cnf" and FolderPath has @"\contrib\openssl")
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !in~ ("system","local service","network service")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, FileName, FolderPath, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc

| where EventType in ("FileCreated","FileModified","FileRenamed")

| where ActorUsername !in~ ("system","local service","network service")
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

lmadmin.exe (Schneider FLM) loads DLL from user-writeable path — CVE-2024-2658 payload execution AIExploitation High
Hunts the moment of exploitation: the lmadminSchneider service (lmadmin.exe, LOCAL SERVICE) loading an attacker DLL referenced by dynamic_path from a world-writeable directory such as C:\Users\Public. lmadmin.exe should only ever load modules from its Schneider Electric install directory and System32, so a load out of Users\Public/ProgramData/Temp/cygwin is the CVE-2024-2658 engine module firing. Rationale: Uses the article's exact exploit mechanic (dynamic_path DLL loaded into lmadmin.exe from a writeable dir like C:\Users\public\malicious.dll) plus the confirmed process identity lmadmin.exe/LOCAL SERVICE from the fetched Process-Hacker screenshot. Sentinel/Datadog/Falcon left blank: image-load telemetry (Sysmon EID7 / FDR ClassifiedModuleLoad) has no clean ASIM table, no listed Datadog sysmon ImageLoaded field, and no listed FDR event_simpleName in this tenant — Defender DeviceImageLoadEvents and raw Sysmon are the viable surfaces. Cross-checked against: • https://community.flexera.com/s/article/cve-2024-2658-flexnet-publisher-potential-local-privilege-escalation-issue • https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2026/06/26105857/schneider-electric2.png
ATT&CKT1574.001 T1574.002 T1068
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate Schneider Electric FLM plugin/engine DLL deployed to a non-standard directory by an admin
  • Third-party OpenSSL engine module intentionally configured by an operator
  • EDR/AV DLL injected into lmadmin.exe for monitoring (loaded from vendor ProgramData path)
  • Software distribution tool staging FLM components under ProgramData before install
Suppression filter
| where not (FolderPath has @"\Schneider Electric\" or SHA256 in (dynamic([])))
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture FolderPath+SHA256 of the loaded DLL; check signer and VirusTotal reputation, and confirm the DLL sits in a user-writeable dir not under \Schneider Electric\. 2) Contain: isolate the host, stop the lmadminSchneider service, and quarantine the DLL; the code already executed as LOCAL SERVICE so treat the host as compromised. 3) Hunt: look for lmadmin.exe spawning shells/LOLBins (potato escalation), for the paired openssl.cnf under the cygwin path, and for credential-theft against the FLM Administration web portal hosted in-process.

| where FolderPath !has @"\Schneider Electric\"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, InitiatingProcessId

lmadmin.exe spawns shell/LOLBin or SYSTEM-integrity child — CVE-2024-2658 potato escalation AIExploitation Medium
Hunts post-exploitation of CVE-2024-2658: the lmadminSchneider license daemon (lmadmin.exe) spawning cmd/PowerShell/LOLBins or a SYSTEM-integrity child. lmadmin.exe is a license web daemon that has no legitimate reason to launch interactive tooling; a shell child — or a child elevated above the parent's LOCAL SERVICE context — indicates the injected DLL abusing SeImpersonatePrivilege (Potato family) to reach NT AUTHORITY\SYSTEM. Rationale: Combines the article's named service binary (lmadmin.exe under LOCAL SERVICE) with the confirmed SeImpersonatePrivilege=Enabled from the fetched screenshot and the article's explicit Potato-family escalation path; a license web daemon spawning a shell or a SYSTEM-integrity child is inherently anomalous and high-fidelity for CVE-2024-2658 exploitation. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/tr/schneider-electric-cve-2024-2658-vulnerability/120436/ • https://media.kasperskycontenthub.com/wp-content/uploads/sites/43/2026/06/26105857/schneider-electric2.png • https://www.cisa.gov/news-events/ics-advisories/icsa-25-037-02
ATT&CKT1134.001 T1068
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Schneider Electric FLM maintenance routine legitimately calling sc.exe or reg.exe during service start
  • Vendor updater invoking cmd.exe under the license service context
  • EDR/monitoring agent injecting into lmadmin.exe and issuing whoami/net for inventory
  • Admin-driven diagnostic script executed through the FLM service during troubleshooting
Suppression filter
| where not (ProcessCommandLine has_any ("FlexNet","lmadmin -","Schneider Electric") or InitiatingProcessFolderPath has @"\Schneider Electric\")
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the child ProcessCommandLine and ProcessIntegrityLevel — a SYSTEM-integrity child from a LOCAL SERVICE parent confirms successful SeImpersonate/Potato escalation. 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the child chain, stop lmadminSchneider, and rotate any credentials (including the FLM Administration portal password) reachable from that host. 3) Hunt: trace descendants for lateral movement to engineering workstations, look for the rogue openssl.cnf/cygwin path and user-writeable DLL that seeded this, and review SYSTEM logons/credential dumps in the window after the spawn.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "lmadmin.exe"
| where FileName has_any ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","net.exe","net1.exe","whoami.exe","rundll32.exe","reg.exe","sc.exe","cscript.exe","wscript.exe","mshta.exe","certutil.exe","bitsadmin.exe")
   or ProcessIntegrityLevel =~ "System"
   or AccountName =~ "system"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessIntegrityLevel, FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessAccountName, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessId
| order by Timestamp desc
SecurityEvent

| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith @"\lmadmin.exe"

| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, TokenElevationType, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Calendly authentication-laundering phish: 'Booking Manager (via Calendly)' lure to hospitality staff AIDelivery Medium
Hunts inbound mail whose sender display name is the campaign's exact 'Booking Manager (via Calendly)' string, relayed through Calendly notification infrastructure to launder SPF/DKIM/DMARC before landing on a Cloudflare-gated .cfd download page. Article-specific because the display name, relay domain and .cfd download destination are all named IOCs. Rationale: Anchored on the article's verbatim sender display name 'Booking Manager (via Calendly)' plus the Calendly relay domain and .cfd download page — high-fidelity because legitimate hotels rarely receive booking notices spoofing that exact display name via Calendly's notification system. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/25/photo-zip-campaign-targeting-hospitality-industry-delivers-node-js-implant-persistent-access/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1566/002/
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1036.005
Data sourcesEmail
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Genuine Calendly booking notifications forwarded into shared reception mailboxes
  • Legitimate marketing or scheduling tools that set a 'via Calendly' display name
  • Internal staff testing a real Calendly integration
  • Security awareness phishing simulations reusing hospitality lures
Suppression filter
| where SenderMailFromAddress !endswith "@calendly.com" or isnotempty(UrlDomain) and UrlDomain endswith ".cfd"
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm SenderMailFromAddress vs SenderDisplayName mismatch and pull the EmailUrlInfo URL chain — flag any redirect ending on a *.cfd or freshly-registered domain. 2) Contain: soft-delete/quarantine the message tenant-wide via the NetworkMessageId, block the .cfd domain at the proxy, and warn the recipient reception accounts. 3) Hunt: pivot any clicking user to DeviceFileEvents for photo-<digits>.zip downloads and to the Node.js implant detections (UC3/UC6) on their device within the next 24h.
EmailEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
| where SenderDisplayName has "Booking Manager (via Calendly)"
| join kind=leftouter (EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain) on NetworkMessageId
| project Timestamp, SenderDisplayName, SenderMailFromAddress, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, Url, UrlDomain
| order by Timestamp desc
// Microsoft Sentinel — auto-derived from the Defender XDR hunt.
// Requires the Microsoft 365 Defender data connector (streams the
// advanced-hunting tables into Sentinel; Timestamp → TimeGenerated).
EmailEvents
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
| where SenderDisplayName has "Booking Manager (via Calendly)"
| join kind=leftouter (EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain) on NetworkMessageId
| project TimeGenerated, SenderDisplayName, SenderMailFromAddress, SenderFromAddress, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, DeliveryAction, Url, UrlDomain
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Photo ZIP campaign delivery artifacts: IMG-/PHOTO-*.png.lnk and photo-<digits>.zip AIDelivery High
Hunts on-disk creation of the campaign's fake-image shortcuts (IMG-<digits>.png.lnk and PHOTO-<digits>.png.lnk) and the photo-<digits>.zip source archives dropped via browser download. Article-specific because both naming patterns and the 1,989-2,079 byte LNK size are named delivery IOCs. Rationale: Uses the article's two exact filename grammars (IMG-/PHOTO-<digits>.png.lnk, photo-<digits>.zip) plus the documented 1,989-2,079 byte LNK size band — a double-extension .png.lnk written by a browser is almost never benign. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/25/photo-zip-campaign-targeting-hospitality-industry-delivers-node-js-implant-persistent-access/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1204/002/
ATT&CKT1204.002 T1036.005
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate ZIPs literally named photo-<digits>.zip from a real photo-sharing workflow
  • Backup/sync tools renaming .lnk shortcuts in Downloads
  • Forensic/IR tooling extracting samples to disk during analysis
  • User-created shortcuts that coincidentally match (rare — double extension .png.lnk is the discriminator)
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("7zfm.exe","winrar.exe") and FileName endswith ".zip")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the .lnk has a double extension and was written by a browser (InitiatingProcessFileName in chrome/msedge/firefox) and that a sibling photo-<digits>.zip exists in Downloads. 2) Contain: isolate the host if the LNK has executed (correlate to powershell.exe child), and quarantine the ZIP+LNK. 3) Hunt: search the same device and account for the Node.js implant path %LOCALAPPDATA%\Nodejs\*.js (UC3) and Add-MpPreference exclusions (UC5).
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where ActionType in ("FileCreated","FileRenamed","FileModified")
| where FileName matches regex @"(?i)^(IMG|PHOTO)-\d+\.png\.lnk$" or FileName matches regex @"(?i)^photo-\d+\.zip$"
| extend SuspectLnkSize = (FileName endswith ".lnk" and FileSize between (1989 .. 2079))
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, FileSize, SuspectLnkSize, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where TargetFileName matches regex @"(?i)^(IMG|PHOTO)-\d+\.png\.lnk$" or TargetFileName matches regex @"(?i)^photo-\d+\.zip$"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, ActingProcessName, ActingProcessCommandLine
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node.js implant: node.exe running random .js from %LOCALAPPDATA%\Nodejs with a domain argument AIInstallation High
Hunts node.exe executing a randomly-named .js script out of %LOCALAPPDATA%\Nodejs — the campaign's resident implant launch pattern, typically followed by a C2 domain argument. Article-specific because the per-user Nodejs script path and script-plus-domain invocation are named in the captured command lines. Rationale: Anchored on the verbatim implant path %LOCALAPPDATA%\Nodejs\<random>.js from the captured node.exe command lines; legitimate Node apps install under Program Files or project dirs, not a randomly-named script in the user's local AppData Nodejs folder. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/25/photo-zip-campaign-targeting-hospitality-industry-delivers-node-js-implant-persistent-access/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1059/007/
ATT&CKT1059.007 T1204.002
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Developer-installed Node tooling that happens to cache scripts under AppData\Local
  • Electron-app helper processes invoking node from a local AppData path
  • nvm/fnm Node version managers running scripts from user AppData
  • Legitimate npm global packages launched via node from a user directory
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("Code.exe","electron.exe") or ProcessCommandLine has_any ("node_modules","nvm","fnm"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the full ProcessCommandLine — confirm a randomly-named .js plus a trailing domain argument (e.g. safedocphoto.info), and capture the node.exe SHA256 and parent process. 2) Contain: isolate the device, kill the node.exe tree, and remove the %LOCALAPPDATA%\Nodejs script and its Run-key persistence. 3) Hunt: pivot to DeviceNetworkEvents for that node.exe reaching .info domains on non-standard ports (UC6) and to Add-MpPreference exclusions (UC5) on the same host.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "node.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has @"\AppData\Local\Nodejs\"
| where ProcessCommandLine has ".js"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine, SHA256
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where TargetProcessName endswith "node.exe"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has @"\AppData\Local\Nodejs\"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has ".js"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessName, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node.js implant persistence: PowerShell detached relauncher (process.execPath + unref) AIInstallation High
Hunts the campaign's persistence relauncher — a powershell.exe one-liner that spawns process.execPath via child_process with {detached:true} and .unref() to keep the Node.js implant resident and hidden across reboots. Article-specific because the exact JS relauncher string fragments are in the captured PowerShell command line. Rationale: Uses three co-occurring literal tokens from the captured relauncher (process.execPath, child_process, unref) inside a powershell.exe command line — this JS-in-PowerShell relaunch shape is essentially unique to the implant and not produced by normal PowerShell usage. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/25/photo-zip-campaign-targeting-hospitality-industry-delivers-node-js-implant-persistent-access/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1547/001/
ATT&CKT1547.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A Node-based dev tool wrapping a relaunch in PowerShell during install (rare)
  • CI/build agents that re-spawn node detached from a PowerShell wrapper
  • Electron auto-update routines that relaunch the host process
  • Legitimate admin scripts copy-pasting Node child_process snippets for testing
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName has_any ("Code.exe","electron.exe") or ProcessCommandLine has "node_modules")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the full PowerShell command and the parent process; confirm it references a %LOCALAPPDATA%\Nodejs script. 2) Contain: kill the powershell/node tree, remove the dropped script, and delete the associated Run-key value pointing at node.exe. 3) Hunt: enumerate HKCU/HKLM Run keys referencing node.exe across the fleet and correlate with the implant execution (UC3) and C2 beaconing (UC6).

| where FileName =~ "powershell.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "process.execPath"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "unref"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "child_process"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where TargetProcessName endswith "powershell.exe"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "process.execPath"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "unref"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "child_process"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName

Defender process-exclusion for %TEMP% executable via Add-MpPreference -ExclusionProcess AIInstallation High
Hunts the campaign's defense-evasion step: powershell.exe invoking Add-MpPreference -ExclusionProcess pointing at a randomly-named EXE under %LOCALAPPDATA%\Temp, carving out the dropped PE payloads from Defender. Article-specific because the exact Add-MpPreference -ExclusionProcess + %TEMP% EXE pattern is in the captured command lines. Rationale: Combines the literal Add-MpPreference -ExclusionProcess flag with a %LOCALAPPDATA%\Temp path (verbatim @-string, no parser ambiguity) from the captured commands — excluding a random Temp EXE from Defender is a near-unambiguous evasion signal. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/25/photo-zip-campaign-targeting-hospitality-industry-delivers-node-js-implant-persistent-access/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1562/001/
ATT&CKT1562.001
Data sourcesEndpoint.Processes
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • IT admin scripting a legitimate AV exclusion for a known dev tool in Temp (rare and usually for Program Files, not Temp)
  • Endpoint management tooling adding temporary exclusions during software deployment
  • Security testers validating Defender exclusion behavior
  • Installer routines that whitelist their own temp extractor
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessFileName in~ (" ccmexec.exe","intunemanagementextension.exe") and InitiatingProcessParentFileName =~ "services.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the excluded EXE path/name and check whether that file exists and its prevalence (DeviceFileEvents) — a randomly-named Temp EXE is the tell. 2) Contain: isolate the host, remove the exclusion (Remove-MpPreference), quarantine the Temp EXE, and run a full scan. 3) Hunt: review all Add-MpPreference exclusions across the fleet for Temp/AppData targets and correlate with the Node.js implant (UC3) and relauncher (UC4) on the same device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(14d)
| where FileName =~ "powershell.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "Add-MpPreference"
| where ProcessCommandLine has "-ExclusionProcess"
| where ProcessCommandLine has @"\AppData\Local\Temp\"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
ImProcessCreate
| where TimeGenerated > ago(14d)
| where TargetProcessName endswith "powershell.exe"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "Add-MpPreference"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has "-ExclusionProcess"
| where TargetProcessCommandLine has @"\AppData\Local\Temp\"
| project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, ActorUsername, TargetProcessCommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Node.js implant C2 beacon to safedocphoto/recallnine/kentjerk/photodoc-secure .info domains AICommand & Control High
Hunts DNS resolution and outbound connections to the campaign's named .info C2 domains, especially when initiated by node.exe over non-standard ports. Article-specific because the four .info domains are confirmed C2 IOCs passed as node.exe arguments. Rationale: Anchored on the four article-confirmed C2 domains plus the discriminator that node.exe is the initiating process — node.exe reaching arbitrary .info hosts on non-standard ports is the campaign's beacon signature. Cross-checked against: • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/security/blog/2026/06/25/photo-zip-campaign-targeting-hospitality-industry-delivers-node-js-implant-persistent-access/ • https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1571/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1571 T1105
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Sinkhole or threat-intel scanners resolving the same .info domains for research
  • Security tooling proactively resolving IOCs for blocklisting
  • A legitimate internal Node service that coincidentally talks to a .info domain (the named domains make this near-zero)
  • DNS logging of a passive resolver pre-caching the domains
Suppression filter
| where RemotePort !in (80, 443) or RemoteUrl has_any ("safedocphoto.info","recallnine.info","kentjerk.info","photodoc-secure.info")
Response runbook

| order by TimeGenerated desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc
NETWORK
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

title: Infostealer - Non-Browser Process Accessing Browser Credential Stores
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d01
status: experimental
description: |
    Stealers (RedLine, Lumma, Vidar, Atomic) read Login Data / cookies
    SQLite databases from Chrome/Edge/Firefox profile directories. Any
    non-browser process touching these files is a high-fidelity signal.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1539/
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1555/003/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1539
    - attack.t1555.003
    - attack.credential-access
logsource:
    category: file_access
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_path:
        TargetFilename|contains:
            - '\Google\Chrome\User Data\'
            - '\Microsoft\Edge\User Data\'
            - '\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\'
    selection_file:
        TargetFilename|endswith:
            - '\Login Data'
            - '\Cookies'
            - '\logins.json'
            - '\cookies.sqlite'
    filter_browser:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\chrome.exe'
            - '\msedge.exe'
            - '\firefox.exe'
            - '\brave.exe'
            - '\opera.exe'
    condition: selection_path and selection_file and not filter_browser
falsepositives:
    - Backup utilities reading user profile files
    - Browser sync agents from non-stock vendors
level: high

        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\logins.json*"
        OR Filesystem.file_path="*\Mozilla\Firefox\Profiles\*\cookies.sqlite*")

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration. → Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s)
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad).
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code.
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key.
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking.
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
Asset exposure — vulnerability matches article CVE(s) Reconnaissance High

Data sourcesVulnerabilities DeviceInfo DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
// TVM tables are daily snapshots — pin a 1-day window so the engine
// partitions on Timestamp instead of scanning the full retention.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-46595", "CVE-2024-45337")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where Timestamp > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId, VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc

// Sentinel has no native TVM table. If MDE→Sentinel streaming is enabled,
// `DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities` is available. Otherwise this UC has
// no Sentinel translation — track via Update / Heartbeat OS-version
// gaps as a coarse approximation.
DeviceTvmSoftwareVulnerabilities
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where CveId in~ ("CVE-2026-46595", "CVE-2024-45337")
| join kind=inner (DeviceInfo | where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)) on DeviceId
| project DeviceName, OSPlatform, CveId,
          VulnerabilitySeverityLevel, RecommendedSecurityUpdate
| order by VulnerabilitySeverityLevel asc, DeviceName asc
